. So in the strict
E_{L}\to 0
limit, the second term, i.e.,
\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};-p)
can be neglected and the last equality is justified.

Similarly, for the RHS of eq.(
151
) we obtain

\begin{split}&\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\bigg[\frac{\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)~\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)-\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)~\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)}{2P_{p}(\eta_{0})}\bigg]\\
&=\frac{1}{2P_{p}(\eta_{0})}\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\bigg[\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{1},k_{2},p)\left(\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},p)-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},-p)\right)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{1},k_{2},p)\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},p)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},-p)\right)\bigg]\,,\end{split}

(153)

in which we have excluded singularities in the contact correlator,
\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};-p)
and in the auxiliary object,
\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};-p)
because of the reason mentioned above.

Now, compairing eq.(
152
) and eq.(
153
) we get

\begin{split}\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};p)=&\frac{1}{2P_{p}(\eta_{0})}\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\bigg[\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{1},k_{2},p)\left(\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},p)-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},-p)\right)\\
&-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{1},k_{2},p)\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},p)+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{3},k_{4},-p)\right)\bigg]\,.\end{split}

(154)

Eq.(
154
) shows that the
4
-point
2
-site scalar correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};p)
can be decomposed into a sum of two factorized contributions composed of lower-point data at the partial energy pole. It is worth highlighting that in the above relation the auxiliary lower-point objects
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
are involved
26
26

26

This application of the cutting rules to obtain the decomposition of the residue at the partial energy singularity was not addressed in
MarinMacedo:2025jco
.
.

Eq.(
154
) can also be generalized to higher-point correlators (involving higher sites in the bulk) as well. Using eq.(
104
), we propose the following decomposition rule for any
r
-site correlator at a partial-energy singularity

\begin{split}&\lim_{E_{L}\to 0}\mathcal{B}^{(r)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},...,\mathbf{k}_{R}\};\{p_{1},...,p_{r-1}\})\\
&=\frac{1}{2P_{p_{r-1}}(\eta_{0})}\bigg[\mathcal{B}^{(r-1)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},...,\mathbf{k}_{M_{r-2}}\},p_{r-1};\{p_{1},..,p_{r-2}\}\right)\text{Disc}_{p_{r-1}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{r-1})\\
&\quad-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(r-1)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},...,\mathbf{k}_{M_{r-2}}\},p_{r-1};\{p_{1},..,p_{r-2}\}\right)\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p_{r-1}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{r-1})\bigg]\,,\end{split}

(155)

where,
E_{L}=k_{L}+\ldots+k_{M_{r-1}}+p_{r-1}
.

With our derived result eq.(
154
) at hand, let us now verify it in a particular example. We consider a
2
-site correlator of conformally coupled scalars with
\phi^{3}
interaction. The
2
-site correlator for this case is given in
arkanihamed2015cosmologicalcolliderphysics
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};p)=\frac{\eta_{0}^{4}(\lambda H)^{2}}{16\,k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}\,p}\Bigg[&\text{Li}_{2}\Big(\frac{k_{L}-p}{k_{L}+k_{R}}\Big)+\text{Li}_{2}\Big(\frac{k_{R}-p}{k_{L}+k_{R}}\Big)\\
&+\log\Big(\frac{k_L+p}{k_L+k_R}\Big)\log\Big(\frac{k_R+p}{k_L+k_R}\Big)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\Bigg]\,,\end{split}

(156)

where
k_{L}=k_{1}+k_{2}
and
k_{R}=k_{3}+k_{4}
. Substituting eq.(
156
) in the LHS of eq.(
154
) we get

\text{LHS}=\lim_{E_{L}\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4};p)=\frac{H^{2}\eta_{0}^{4}\lambda^{2}}{16\,k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}\,p}\Bigg[\log(k_L+p)\,\log\Big(\frac{k_R+p}{k_R-p}\Big)\Bigg]\,.

(157)

Next, to evaluate the RHS of eq.(
154
), we require the corresponding contact correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}
and the auxiliary object
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
.
We first consider the contact wave-function coefficient which is IR-divergent and given by

\psi^{(1)}_{\phi^{3}}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)=\frac{i\lambda}{H^{4}\eta_{0}^{3}}\log\big((k_L+p)\eta_0\big)+\frac{\lambda\pi}{2H^{4}\eta_{0}^{3}}\,.

(158)

From this, we obtain

\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(k_{1},k_{2},p)=\frac{\lambda\pi H^{2}\eta_{0}^{3}}{8\,k_{1}k_{2}p}\,,

(159)

and, using the definition in eq.(
77
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(k_{1},k_{2},p)=\frac{i\,H^{2}\eta_{0}^{3}\lambda}{4k_{1}k_{2}p}\,\log\big((k_1+k_2+p)\eta_0\big)\,.

(160)

The RHS of eq.(
154
) can now be expressed as

\begin{split}\text{RHS}&=\lim_{E_{L}\rightarrow 0}\Bigg[\frac{H^{2}\eta_{0}^{4}\lambda^{2}\pi^{2}}{32\,k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}p}+\frac{H^{2}\eta_{0}^{4}\lambda^{2}}{16\,k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}p}\,\log((k_1+k_2+p)\eta_0)\log\Big(\frac{k_3+k_4+p}{k_3+k_4-p}\Big)\Bigg]\\
&=\lim_{E_{L}\rightarrow 0}\frac{H^{2}\eta_{0}^{4}\lambda^{2}}{16\,k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}p}\,\log(k_L+p)\,\log\Big(\frac{k_R+p}{k_R-p}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(161)

Comparing eqs. (
157
) and (
161
), we see a perfact match between the LHS and RHS of eq.(
154
), furthermore, confirming that the
4
-point
2
-site correlator can be decomposed as eq.(
154
) at the partial-energy pole.

It is worth noting that this conformally coupled
\phi^{3}
theory is an example where both
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
appear at the same order in the leading late-time, i.e. at
\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}^{3})
. Also, this decomposition of the residue at partial energy singularity serves as a consistency of our discontinuity relation derived previously. Most importantly, we must take note of the significance of the auxiliary objects
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
which are not only present and play a crucial role in the discontinuity relation and also in the decomposition of the partial energy residues for the cosmological correlators.

8
Conclusions and outlook

In this section, we will conclude by discussing the implications of our main results and highlighting the novelties thereof. Following that, we will also comment on some possible future directions.

First, we briefly summarise the technical results in this paper and their significance:

•

In eq.(
60
) we have obtained the discontinuity of the bulk-to-bulk propagators in the in-in picture using Hermitian analyticity written in terms of the bulk-to-boundary propagators (see eq.(
34
)) and unitarity valid for conformally coupled and massless scalar fields in
4
-dimensional de Sitter space-time.

•

In eq.(
81
) we have derived the single-cut discontinuity relation for a tree-level correlator with
2
-sites or vertices, working with conformally coupled scalar fields with polynomial
\phi^{n}
type interactions. The discontinuity of the higher point correlator (written on the LHS of eq.(
81
)) gets decomposed into discontinuities of the lower-point correlator (denoted by
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L};p)
) and lower point auxiliary objects (denoted by
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)
). These auxiliary objects are not exactly cosmological correlators but can be written in terms of imaginary pieces of the lower-point wave-function coefficients (see eq.(
77
)). Also, on the RHS of eq.(
81
), we have obtained two types of discontinuity operation - the ‘
\text{Disc}_{p}
’ operator acts on the standard correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L};p)
, and the other operator ‘
\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p}
’ acts on the auxiliary objects
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)
. These operators are defined in eq.(
25
) and eq.(
26
) respectively.

•

In eq.(
104
) we have generalised our single-cut discontinuity relation to a cosmological correlator with generic
r
-sites at tree-level, again focussing on conformally coupled and massless scalar field theory with
\phi^{n}
interactions.

•

That the auxiliary objects
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(m)}
appear in the discontinuity relations for the cosmological correlators is one of the most significant findings of our paper. We have checked that for conformally coupled scalar interactions
\phi^{n}
, which are IR-convergent, i.e.,
n\geq 4
, depending on whether
n
is even or odd, one or the other term on the RHS of eq.(
81
) contributes to the leading late-time limit. More specifically, for
n=
even, the product of discontinuities of the lower point correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}
dominates as
\eta_{0}\to 0^{-}
over the other term involving
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
’s. On the contrary, for
n=
odd, the product of
\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p}
’s acting on the lower-point auxiliary counterparts
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
is the dominant contribution that matches exactly with the discontinuity of the
2
-site correlator on the LHS of eq.(
81
).

We have made similar observations for tree-level diagrams with more than
2
-sites as well, where for
\phi^{n}
interactions with
n=\text{even}
, the product of discontinuities of the lower point correlators
\mathcal{B}
dominates at the late time limit,
\eta_{0}\to 0^{-}
. However, for
n=\text{odd}
, contributions from both the lower point correlators and the auxiliary objects contribute. To be precise, e.g., following eq.(
218
) and (
219
) we see that for a
3
-site correlator, with
n=\text{odd}
the product of discontinuity of the lower point correlators and the auxiliary objects scale at the same order in
\eta_{0}
at the late time limit. Hence, both of them must be taken into account.

Why the discontinuity relation works in this peculiar way in the leading late-time limit, depending on whether
n
is even or odd in a
\phi^{n}
theory, is an interesting question. Still, at this point, we lack a deeper understanding of this.

•

In Appendix
C
, we derive the discontinuity relation for the
2
-site tree-level correlator, staying entirely within the wave-function picture, starting from the cutting rules already known for the wave-function coefficients. In the final result eq.(
197
) we see that the auxiliary counterparts of the correlator, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
’s are also present. This derivation provides further justification for the discontinuity relation obtained using the in-in picture. However, as far as extending this wave-function picture-based derivation to more general
r
-site correlator or loop diagrams is concerned, we think our derivation based on the in-in picture is more controlled and less technically involved.

•

In §
5
, we have generalized the discontinuity relation to massless scalars, cases with derivative interactions, and also to loop diagrams. In eq.(
119
) we have derived the discontinuity relation for
2
-site
1
-loop correlators with
\phi^{n}
interactions. Based on an intuitive understanding of the structures, in eq.(
120
) we have conjectured a discontinuity relation for a
2
-site correlator but at the arbitrary
\ell
-loop order.

•

Using our discontinuity relation, we have also gained insight about the analytic pole-structure of cosmological correlators. In eq.(
154
) we have derived the partial energy singularity of a
2
-site correlator. This equation demonstrates that in the limit when the total energy flowing in an internal site or vertex (i.e., the sum of the magnitude of individual momentum in the external legs and the internal line attached to that vertex) vanishes, the correlator develops a simple pole, and the residue gets decomposed into lower point objects and the discontinuity of them.

•

As a check of principle, we have derived and checked the discontinuity relation for the flat space cosmological correlator at the tree-level in Appendix
G
. It highlights the differences that one notices when we derive the discontinuity relation for de Sitter.

•

At various relevant places throughout the bulk of this paper, we have highlighted the similarities and differences of our analysis with
MarinMacedo:2025jco
, which we learned about while our project was nearing completion. To summarise, in terms of the basic idea and the strategy, such as the in-in propagator identities and the tree-level discontinuity relations, there are similarities. However, our treatment of the loop diagrams is different. Furthermore, at the level of executing the strategy and various final interpretations of the discontinuity relations, our work provides further refinements and physical understanding.

Next, we discuss some future directions that, in our opinion, will be worth pursuing.

•

We have seen that the discontinuity relation for cosmological correlators gets decomposed into a sum of products of lower-point data. However, do we call them a factorization of the discontinuity relation? There might be a better basis of variables where the discontinuity relation for the cosmological correlators gets factorized in the true sense of its meaning. Another related interesting direction is to reformulate the cutting rule using alternative kinematic variables, such as spinor-helicity or twistor variables. Recent work has shown that cosmological (A)dS correlators, especially those involving spin, can be written in twistor space with remarkable simplification
Baumann:2025twist
;
David:2019dSspinor
. Such a reformulation could expose hidden simplicity, making kinematic constraints more manifest, and offer a more intuitive understanding of analytic structures, potentially including loop corrections. The factorisation aspect may also manifest when we use these alternative kinematic variables.

•

In a recent work, it has been argued that cosmological correlators can be computed from flat-space Feynman diagrams. In several cases, this approach becomes easier than the existing in-in or wave-function approaches. This method is known as “dressing rules”
Chowdhury:2025ohm
;
Chowdhury:2025nnk
. Although an algorithm to determine the dressing rules for a particular cosmological correlator was developed, a basic understanding of why this fantastic idea of dressing Feynman rules yields the cosmological correlators remained a mystery. It would have been great if the dressing rules could be derived from fundamental principles, such as the optical theorem and cutting rules. We have been able to reproduce the dressing rules using the single-cut discontinuity relation, which has been formulated in this paper based on inputs from principles such as unitarity and hermitian analyticity. We will report this in detail in an upcoming work
DKKK
.

•

In this work, we restricted our attention to scalar operators. A natural next step is to extend the single-cut discontinuity framework to spinning fields. The so-called cosmological bootstrap has demonstrated that wave-function coefficients with spin‑
1
and spin‑
2
exchanges can be systematically built using symmetry and factorization methods from simpler scalar “seed” solutions
Baumann:2020dch
. Similarly, by embedding spinning correlators into our cutting-rule framework, we can clarify how unitarity and analytic-structure constraints structure interactions involving spins.

•

Recent work on spinning “unparticle” exchange at tree level
Pimentel:2025rds
shows that unparticle propagators have enhanced symmetries, causing cosmological correlators to follow differential equations. An interesting question is whether a similar single-cut rule can be established for these propagators.

•

In
Chakraborty:2023los
, the authors introduced a framework for cosmological correlators in quantum gravity, noting the lack of local gauge-invariant observables, which sets it apart from conventional QFT. They observed that cosmological correlators in the Bunch-Davies vacuum do not exhibit increased symmetry beyond the solutions of the Wheeler–DeWitt equation. In follow-up work
Chakraborty:2025izq
, they examined correlators in a general excited state as a perturbation of the Bunch-Davies vacuum. It will be interesting to explore if the states in
Chakraborty:2023los
can be formulated within the single-cut discontinuity relation and how unitarity appears in this framework.

•

An interesting direction is to extend our cutting rule to cosmological correlators computed in non‑standard initial states related to the Bunch–Davies vacuum by a Bogoliubov transformation. Previous studies have shown that such vacua lead to additional analytic features (folded or collinear singularities) in late-time correlators
Shukla:2016bnu
;
Jain:2022uja
;
Chopping:2024bog
;
Jain:2022uja
;
Ghosh:2024aqd
;
Ansari:2024pgq
;
Ghosh:2025pxn
. Investigating the applicability of cutting rules in this context would test the robustness of unitarity constraints and partial-energy factorization. It could provide new insights into the analytic structure of correlators beyond the standard vacuum.

•

Although the single-cut discontinuity relations we have derived in this paper are valid for any order in the perturbation theory, we want to make it clear that to derive them, we have imposed the unitarity constraint, which is perturbative in principle. It would be interesting to derive a set of single-cut discontinuity relations solely from the non-perturbative setting discussed in
Loparco:2023rug
;
Hogervorst:2021uvp
;
SalehiVaziri:2024joi
.

Appendix A
Notation and convention

In this appendix we summarise various short-hand notations that we defined in the bulk of this paper. These definitions have been repeatedly used in the technical calculations throughout the paper.

\bullet
For a polynomial interaction of type
\phi^{n}
we have used the following shorthand notation:

\begin{split}&\text{Left most vertex}:\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(2\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(k_{i})\right)=\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}~\eqref{PkL PkR})\\
&\text{Right most vertex}:\prod_{i=(r-1)(n-2)+2}^{r(n-2)+2}\left(2\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(k_{i})\right)=\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{R}}(\eta_{0})~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}~\eqref{PkL PkR})\\
&\text{Any $j$-th vertex in the middle}:\prod_{i=j(n-2)+2}^{(j+1)(n-2)+1}\left(2\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(k_{i})\right)=\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{M_{j}}}(\eta_{0})~~~(\text{defined in eq.}~\eqref{PkM})\end{split}

(162)

where
r
is the number of vertices in the bulk.

\bullet
For the 2 point wave-function coefficient we use (e.g. see eq.(
16
))

\begin{split}&\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(k_{i})=\psi_{2}(k_{i})=\frac{k_{i}}{H^{2}\eta_{0}^{2}}=\left(2P_{k_{i}}(\eta_{0})\right)^{-1}\\
&\text{Power spectrum}:P_{k}(\eta_{0})=\frac{1}{2\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(k)}\end{split}

(163)

\bullet
For the objects at
r
-site we will use superscript
r

\begin{split}&\text{Correlator}:\mathcal{B}^{(r)}\left(\{k_{i}\};\{p_{i}\}\right)~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{see e.g. eq.}\eqref{corr 1 2 def})\\
&\text{Auxiliary object}:\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(r)}\left(\{k_{i}\};\{p_{i}\}\right)~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{see e.g. eq.}\eqref{corrt 1 2 def})\\
&\text{Wavefunction coefficient}:\psi^{(r)}\left(\{k_{i}\};\{p_{i}\}\right)~~~~~(\text{see e.g. eq.}\eqref{mathfrak B1})\end{split}

(164)

\bullet
For the time integral we use

\begin{split}&\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\int_{-\infty}^{\eta_{0}}\frac{d\eta_{j}}{H^{4}\eta_{j}^{4}}\right)=\int^{(n)}d\eta~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}\eqref{n time integrals})\\
&\int(d\eta_{r})=\int_{-\infty}^{\eta_{0}}\frac{d\eta_{r}}{(H\eta_{r})^{4}}~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}\eqref{rth time integral})\end{split}

(165)

\bullet
For the product of bulk-to-boundary propagators for correlator we will use

\begin{split}&\text{Left most vertex}:\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}K_{k_{i}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta^{\prime})=\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta^{\prime}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}~\eqref{defmathcalK})\\
&\text{Right most vertex}:\prod_{i=(r-1)(n-2)+2}^{r(n-2)+2}K_{k_{i}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta^{\prime})=\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta^{\prime}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}~\eqref{defmathcalK})\\
&\text{Any $j$-th vertex in the middle}:\prod_{i=j(n-2)+2}^{(j+1)(n-2)+1}K_{k_{i}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta^{\prime})=\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta^{\prime}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M_{j}}\})~~(\text{used in eq.}~\eqref{3 site from inin})\end{split}

(166)

\bullet
Conversion between bulk-to-boundary propagators for correlator and that of the wave-function

\begin{split}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}K_{k_{i}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{i})K_{p_{j}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{i})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})P_{p_{j}}^{-1}(\eta_{0})}\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{i}}^{\psi}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},p_{j}\})~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{defined in eq.}~\eqref{relation mathcalK})\end{split}

(167)

where in the RHS, the superscript
\psi
stands for the wave-function coefficient.

Appendix B
Scalars with generic masses: analytic continuation, discontinuity relation

In the bulk of the paper we have focused on the special cases with half-integer
\nu
, in particular the conformally coupled and massless scalars. In this appendix we discuss the analytic continuation to the magnitude of the 3-momentum, discontinuity operation and the discontinuity relation for a
2
-site correlator with the generic
\nu
case.

From eq.(
3
), the mode function for a free scalar of mass
m
in
dS_{4}
is given as

\begin{split}f_{k}^{-}(\eta)&=\frac{-i\sqrt{\pi}}{2}H\,e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(-\eta)^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)\,,\\
f_{k}^{+}(\eta)&=\frac{i\sqrt{\pi}}{2}H\,e^{\frac{-i\pi}{2}\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(-\eta)^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)\,,\end{split}

(168)

where,
\nu
is given by

\nu=\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}-\frac{m^{2}}{H^{2}}}\,.

(169)

Using the expressions mode functions, we get the power spectrum as following

P_{k}(\eta_{0})=\lim_{\eta_{0}\rightarrow 0}|f_{k}^{-}(\eta_{0})|^{2}=H\,\eta_{0}^{3/2}\Bigg[\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,(-k\eta_{0})^{\nu}}{2^{1+\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)}\left(1+i\cot(\pi\nu)\right)-\frac{i(-k\eta_{0})^{-\nu}}{\sqrt{\pi}\,2^{1-\nu}\Gamma(\nu)}\Bigg]+\cdots\,.

(170)

For light mass fields where
m^{2}<\frac{9}{4}H^{2}
,
\nu
is real and positive. In this case, the first term in brackets of eq.(
170
) approaches zero faster than the second
and can be neglected. So the power spectrum becomes

P_{k}(\eta_{0})=\frac{H^{2}}{\pi^{2}2^{2(\nu-1)}\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}(-\eta_{0})^{3-2\nu}\,k^{-2\nu}\,,\qquad\text{for}\quad m^{2}<\tfrac{9}{4}H^{2}\,.

(171)

The second case is when fields are massive and

m^{2}>\frac{9}{4}H^{2}
, so that
\nu
becomes complex. In this case both of the two
terms inside the brackets of eq.(
170
) are of the same order.
The resulting power spectrum oscillates while decaying as
\eta_{0}^{3}
.

Now, let us focus on convergence of any bulk-time integration for general massive field. Using the asymptotic form of Hankel functions in
DLMF
, we can write following from eq.(
168
)

\begin{split}&f_{k}^{-}(\eta)\sim\frac{\,H\eta\,e^{-ik\eta}}{\sqrt{2k}}\,,\qquad f_{k}^{+}(\eta)\sim\frac{\,H\eta\,e^{ik\eta}}{\sqrt{2k}}\,,\qquad\text{as}~\eta\rightarrow-\infty\,.\end{split}

(172)

Since the in-in propagators are constructed from the mode functions, we can write their asymptotic behaviours readily using eq,(
172
). Let us write down the asymptotic early time behavior of in-in bulk-to-boundary propagators, defined in eq.(
7
),

\begin{split}&\lim_{\eta\rightarrow-\infty}K_{k}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta)=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow-\infty}\bigg(f_{k}^{-}(\eta_{0})f_{k}^{+}(\eta)\bigg)\sim\eta\,e^{ik\eta}\,,\\
&\lim_{\eta\rightarrow-\infty}K_{k}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta)=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow-\infty}\bigg(f_{k}^{+}(\eta_{0})f_{k}^{-}(\eta)\bigg)\sim\eta\,e^{-ik\eta}\,.\end{split}

(173)

This shows oscillatory behavior at early time (
\eta\rightarrow-\infty
) and this should be regularized by some damping factor to ensure the convergence of bulk time integral running from
-\infty
to 0. Usually this is done by rotating the conformal time corrdinate
\eta\rightarrow\eta(1\pm i\epsilon^{\prime})
(with
\epsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow 0^{+}
). We can achieve the same without rotating
\eta
-coordinate but instead analytically continuing momenta,
k\rightarrow(k\pm i\epsilon)
(with
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
). From the asymptotic expressions of in-in bulk-to-boundary propagators in eq.(
173
), we realize that the convergence of bulk time integral requires that we analytically continue the momenta of
K_{k}^{+}
to the lower-half complex k-plane. Similarly, the momenta of
K_{k}^{-}
needs to be analytically continued to the upper-half complex k-plane. For convergence of bulk-time integral, we will define the Hankel functions as function of the following momenta

\begin{split}&\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}\left(-k^{-}\eta\right)\quad;\,\text{such that}~~k^{-}=(k-i\epsilon)\,,~\text{and}\\
&\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}\left(-k^{+}\eta\right)\quad;\,\text{such that}~~k^{+}=(k+i\epsilon)\,.\end{split}

(174)

Now, we focus on analytically continuing the mode functions from the positive
k
-axis to the negative
k
-axis
27
27

27

We will analytically continue only the magnitude of the cut-line (any internal line) momentum to the negative
k
-axis.

. This continuation is subtle because the Hankel functions
\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)
and
\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)
for generic
\nu
(not half-integer) have a branch cut along the negative real axis,
k\in(-\infty,0)
, in the complex
k
-plane.

To maintain convergence of the Hankel functions throughout the analytic continuation, we must specify whether the continuation approaches the branch cut from above or below. Using eq. (
174
), we find that
\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}
should be continued from
k\to-k
from below, while
\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}
should be continued from above
28
28

28

The conjugation in the analytic continuation implies

k^{+}\rightarrow e^{i\pi}(k^{+})^{*}\implies(k+i\epsilon)\rightarrow(-k+i\epsilon),

indicating continuation in the upper half of the complex
k
-plane. Similarly, the analytic continuation of
k^{-}
occurs in the lower half of the complex
k
-plane.
.

\begin{split}&\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(-k^{+}\eta)\xrightarrow{k^{+}\rightarrow e^{i\pi}(k^{+})^{*}}\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}((k^{+})^{*}\eta)\,,\qquad\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(-k^{-}\eta)\xrightarrow{k^{-}\rightarrow e^{-i\pi}(k^{-})^{*}}\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}((k^{-})^{*}\eta)\,.\end{split}

(175)

Now, we can use the following properties of Hankel functions
DLMF

\begin{split}&\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(z\,e^{i\pi})=-e^{-\nu\pi i}\,\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(z)\,,\qquad\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(z\,e^{-i\pi})=-e^{\nu\pi i}\,\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(z)\,.\end{split}

(176)

Using the relations written above the following property of bulk-to-boundary propagator in wave-function picture can be found

\begin{split}&\left(K^{\psi}_{-k^{*}}(\eta_{0},\eta)\right)^{*}=K_{k}^{\psi}(\eta_{0},\eta)\,,\qquad\text{or, equivalently,}\qquad K^{\psi}_{-k^{*}}(\eta_{0},\eta)=\left(K_{k}^{\psi}(\eta_{0},\eta)\right)^{*}\,,\end{split}

(177)

which can be verified using the expression of bulk-to-boundary propgator in eq. eq.(
17
) and the property eq.(
176
). The relation in eq.(
177
) was mentioned as Hermitian analyticity in
Goodhew:2020hob
, which we have also used in eq.(
30
).

Furthermore, using the relation between bulk-to-boundary propagators in the wave-function picture and the in-in picture, eq.(
19
), we can write eq.(
177
) in terms of the in-in bulk-to-boundary propagators

\begin{split}&K_{-k^{*}}^{\mp}(\eta_{0},\eta)=K_{k}^{\pm}(\eta_{0},\eta)\frac{P_{-k^{*}}(\eta_{0})}{P_{k}(\eta_{0})}\,,\end{split}

(178)

where
P_{k}(\eta_{0})
is given in eq. (
170
). From the expression of
P_{k}(\eta_{0})
, we observe that a branch point can occur at
k=0
for certain values of
\nu
. In all such cases, the multivaluedness of
P_{-k^{*}}(\eta_{0})
can be resolved by analytically continuing from below. Eq. (
178
) can then be interpreted as the statement of Hermitian analyticity in terms of the in-in bulk-to-boundary propagators, corresponding to eq. (
33
) where we restrict to real momenta.

Now, we can substitute eq.(
178
) in the expressions of the bulk-to-bulk propagators written in the in-in picture in eq.(
8
) and obtain

\begin{split}&G^{++}_{-p^{*}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=G^{--}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\,\mathcal{R}\quad,\quad G^{+-}_{-p^{*}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=G^{-+}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\,\mathcal{R}\,,\\
&G^{-+}_{-p^{*}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=G^{+-}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\,\mathcal{R}\quad,\quad G^{--}_{-p^{*}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=G^{++}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})/\,\mathcal{R}\,,\end{split}

(179)

where,
\mathcal{R}
is given by

\mathcal{R}=\frac{P_{p}(\eta_{0})}{P_{-p^{*}}(\eta_{0})}\,.

(180)

Note that eq.(
179
) is the statement of Hermitian analyticity written in terms of bulk-to-bulk in-in propagators and is the generalisation of eq.(
35
) for generic
\nu
.

Following the same steps as in §
3
we can argue that the following relations can be derived

G^{\pm\pm}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})+\mathcal{R}\,G^{\pm\pm}_{-p^{*}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\frac{K_{p}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})K_{p}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})K_{p}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})}{P_{p}(\eta_{0})}\,,

(181)

which are the statements of Hermitian analyticity and unitarity written in terms of bulk-to-bulk in-in propagators and are the generalisation of eq.(
60
) which has a restricted validity for half-integer
\nu
.

Applying these modifications to the derivation of the cutting rule for the
2
-site correlator, we obtain the following result, which holds for scalars with arbitrary generic
\nu

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)+\,\mathcal{R}\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p^{*})=\\
&\frac{1}{2\,P_{p}(\eta_{0})}\bigg[\bigg(\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)+\,\mathcal{R}\,\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)+\,\mathcal{R}\,\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p^{*})\bigg)\\
&\qquad-\bigg(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-\,\mathcal{R}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)-\,\mathcal{R}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p^{*})\bigg)\bigg]\,.\end{split}

(182)

Note that this is the discontinuity relation involving a
2
-site cosmological correlator in a theory of scalar fields but with generic masses.

If we restrict ourselves to scalar fields with integer valued
\nu
(that includes conformally coupled and massless scalars), using eq.(
171
), eq.(
180
), we get
\mathcal{R}=-1
. Substituting
\mathcal{R}=-1
, the LHS of eq.(
182
) reduces to

\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p^{*})\,.

(183)

By redefining the complex momentum
p
as
p=i\,q
(with
q\in\mathbb{R}
)
29
29

29

This treatment has also been done for interpreting cosmological optical theorem of wave-function coefficients in
Goodhew:2020hob
.
, we can write

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p^{*})\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};i\,q-\epsilon)-\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};i\,q+\epsilon)\Big]=\text{Disc}\!\left[\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};i\,q)\right].\end{split}

(184)

This shows that, for scalar fields with half-integer
\nu
, our discontinuity operation for correlators with complex momenta reduces to the standard notion of discontinuity in complex analysis.

For scalar fields with half-integer
\nu
(including the massless and conformally coupled cases), the Hankel functions
\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)
and
\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)
for
\nu=n+\tfrac{1}{2}
(with
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
) are defined on the principal branch
DLMF
as follows,

\begin{split}\mathbb{H}^{(1)}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(z)&=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi z}}\,e^{iz}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{i^{\,(k-n-1)}\,(n+k)!}{k!\,(n-k)!\,(2z)^{k}},\\
\mathbb{H}^{(2)}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(z)&=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi z}}\,e^{-iz}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-i)^{\,(k-n-1)}\,(n+k)!}{k!\,(n-k)!\,(2z)^{k}}\,.\end{split}

(185)

From these series expansions and eq. (
7
), we see that the in-in bulk-to-boundary propagators have a pole only at
k=0
for scalar fields with half-integer
\nu
30
30

30

For half-integer
\nu
,
\mathbb{H}^{(1,2)}_{\nu}(-k\eta)
include a factor of
\sqrt{k}
, which is multivalued along the negative
k
-axis. But, in-in propagators are built from products of two mode functions, making them single-valued on the negative
k
-axis.

. This eliminates any ambiguity in analytically continuing the in-in propagators to the negative
k
axis. In such cases, the correlator can be continued to the negative
k
-axis without introducing
i\epsilon
shifts, which allows us to write eq. (
183
) as follows.

\text{LHS of eq.\eqref{discb2tgeneric}}=\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p)\,.

(186)

According, to our definition in eq.(
25
), this becomes

\text{Disc}_{p}\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)=\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\,\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p)\,.

One might worry that subtleties could still arise for half-integer
\nu
in IR-divergent settings. However, analytically continuing the internal energy to negative values does not introduce any multivaluedness, even when IR divergence is present
31
31

31

For example, analytically continuing the internal energy to negative values sends
\log(k_1+k_2+p_1)\to\log(k_1+k_2-p_1)
, where
p_{1}=|\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}|
. This remains single valued because the argument of the logarithm stays positive by the triangle inequality.
in the half-integer
\nu
cases.

The preceding set of arguments also justifies removing the complex conjugation on the momenta and writing the cutting rule for the correlator as in eq.(
81
). It also suggests that our cutting rule for the exchange correlator in eq.(
81
) remains valid in IR-divergent scenarios, provided
\nu
is a half-integer.

The situation is more subtle for fields with non–half-integer
\nu
, where we could not find any closed-form expression for the bulk time integral. In these cases, one must follow our analytic continuation prescription, explicitly approaching the negative-
k
axis from below to fix the branch structure of
\mathcal{R}
. We expect eq. (
182
) to hold in this setting, although it has not been explicitly tested.

Appendix C
Discontinuity of
2
-site correlator using wave-function cutting rules

In this appendix, we show that the discontinuity relation that we have obtained from the in-in picture in §
4.1
, can also be derived from the wave-function formalism. In particular, the strategy we will follow is that we will first write a
2
-site tree-level correlator in terms of the wave-function coefficients. In the next step, we will use the definition of the discontinuity operation and then by using the cutting-rules derived in
Goodhew:2021oqg
at the wave-function level we will derive the discontinuity relation for the correlator.

For convenience, we will restrict ourselves to the following polynomial self-interaction of conformally coupled scalar field written in eq.(
61
).
Although our single-cut rule perfectly works for the IR divergent interaction, in the current context to avoid the technical complications, we will restrict to
n\geq 4
which describes the IR-convergent interaction. In this theory, a
2
-site correlator can be expressed in terms of the wave-function coefficients as follows

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)=\frac{2\left(\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)~\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)}{\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p)}\right)}{\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{R}}(\eta_{0})}\,,\end{split}

(187)

where
p
is the bulk-to-bulk momenta and
\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\}=\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{n-1}\}
,
\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\}=\{k_{n},\cdots,k_{2n-2}\}
.
Applying the discontinuity operation eq.(
25
) in the
2
-site correlator, by definition we obtain the following expression that we need to expand in terms of the wave-function coefficients

\begin{split}\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)=\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p)\,.\end{split}

(188)

Employing eq.(
187
) we can write the discontinuity of the
2
-site correlator as follows

\begin{split}&\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{k_{i}\};p)=\frac{2}{\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{R}}(\eta_{0})}\Bigg(\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p)\\
&+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)~\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)}{\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p)}+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)~\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p)}{\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p)}\Bigg)\,.\end{split}

(189)

At this point we have to make use of the cutting rules
Goodhew:2020hob
;
Goodhew:2021oqg
;
Goodhew:2023bcu
for the wave-function coefficients. Note that the terms in the first line on the RHS of the above equation can be written as follows

\begin{split}\mathbb{R}e\Bigg(\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p)\Bigg)=\mathbb{R}e\Bigg(\text{Disc}_{p}\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)\Bigg)\,.\end{split}

(190)

Cutting rule for a tree level wave-function coefficient suggests that the discontinuity of the exchange diagram can be thought of as a product of the discontinuity of two contact level wave-function coefficients as follows
32
32

32

Our convention of the Disc. is slightly different from the convention used in
Goodhew:2020hob
. However, for a conformally coupled and real scalar polynomial interaction the final result of the discontinuity of a wave-function coefficient turns out to be same in both the conventions.

\begin{split}\text{Disc}_{p}\psi^{(2)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p\right)=-\frac{1}{2\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p)}\text{Disc}_{p}\psi^{(1)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p\right)\text{Disc}_{p}\psi^{(1)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p\right)\,.\end{split}

(191)

For a contact correlator, from the definition of discontinuity we obtain the following

\begin{split}&\text{Disc}_{p}\psi^{(1)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p\right)=\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\\
=&\Big(\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\Big)+\Big(i\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-i\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\Big)\,.\end{split}

(192)

Substituting eq.(
192
) and eq.(
191
) in eq.(
190
), after some simple algebraic simplification, yields the following expression

\begin{split}&\mathbb{R}e\Bigg(\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)-\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};-p)\Bigg)=-\frac{1}{2\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p)}\times\\
&\Big[\Big(\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\Big)\Big(\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)-\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p)\Big)+\\
&\Big(i\,\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-i\,\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\Big)\Big(i\,\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)-i\,\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p)\Big)\Big]\,.\end{split}

(193)

With the above expression, the RHS of the eq.(
189
) cab be simplified and put in the following form

\begin{split}\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{k_{i}\};p)=&\frac{2}{\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{R}}(\eta_{0})P_{p}^{-1}(\eta_{0})}\times\\
&\Bigg(\Big(\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)+\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\Big)\Big(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\}\to\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\}\Big)\\
&-\Big(i\,\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)-i\,\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p)\Big)\Big(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\}\to\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\}\Big)\Bigg).\end{split}

(194)

At this level, it is worth highlting that in the RHS of the above equation, the first line inside the parenthesis will contribute for
n=
even interaction only. This happens because a contact wave-function coefficient is completely real only for conformally coupled even interaction. On the other hand, the second line inside the parenthesis will contribute for
n=
odd since a contact wave-function is completely imaginary for conformally coupled odd interaction.

The above expression can be further written in terms of the contact correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}\},p)
and the auxiliary object
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}\},p)
we have encountered before. Note that, a contact correlator can be expressed in terms of contact wave-function coefficient by the following relation

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)=\frac{2~\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)}{\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})P_{p}^{-1}(\eta_{0})}\,,\end{split}

(195)

and recall that we defined

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)=\frac{2i~\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)}{\mathcal{P}^{-1}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})P_{p}^{-1}(\eta_{0})}\,.

(196)

By using eq.(
195
) and eq.(
196
) we can finally rewrite the eq.(
194
) as follows

\begin{split}{\text{Disc}}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p)=&\frac{1}{2P_{p}(\eta_{0})}\Bigg(\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)\times\text{Disc}_{p}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)\\
&-\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p)\times\widetilde{\text{Disc}}_{p}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p)\Bigg)\,,\end{split}

(197)

which is essentially the single-cut rule we have derived in eq.(
81
) starting directly from the in-in perspective of
2
-site correlator.

Appendix D
A single-cut discontinuity relation for
3
-site tree-level correlator

Here we will derive the single-cut rule for 3-site correlator for the polynomial interaction written in eq.(
61
).

A 3-site correlator contains 3 vertices, so there are
8
possible ways to label the vertices as
``+"
or
``-"
. Therefore the full correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(3)}
is the sum of the contributions from each of these configurations, which is expressed as follows

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}^{(3)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2}\right)\\
&=(i\lambda)^{3}\int^{(3)}d\eta\Bigg[\Bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{++}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{+-}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{-+}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\\
&+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{--}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\Bigg)\\
&-\Bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{++}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{+-}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{-+}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\\
&+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})G^{--}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\Bigg)\Bigg]\end{split}

(198)

Note that,
\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\}
describes the collection of the external energies at the internal vertex of the diagram. And
\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\}
,
\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\}
as usual denote the collection of external energies at the left and right most vertex.

Applying discontinuity with respect to
p_{2}
and following the propagator identities eq.(
60
) we can write

\begin{split}&\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2}\right)=(i\lambda)^{3}\int^{(3)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\bigg)\\
&\bigg(\frac{K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})+K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}\bigg)\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(199)

The left-most term in the last line can again be written in the same form as given in eq.(
65
) and then we obtain the following two terms that we have to analyze further

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-1}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{3}}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\int^{(3)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\bigg)\\
&\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})+K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})\Big)\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(200)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-2}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{3}}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\int^{(3)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\bigg)\\
&\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})-K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})-K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})\Big)\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(201)

Now we will analyze the term-1 in the following. Note that the
\eta_{3}
integral in eq.(
200
) can be handled in the similar way as given in eq.(
71
)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-1}}^{\eta_{3}-\text{part}}\\
&=(i\lambda)\int d\eta_{3}\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})+K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})\Big)\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\\
&=\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p_{2})~~~~\equiv~~~~\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\end{split}

(202)

Next, the part involving
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
in eq.(
200
) can be written as follows just by using the property of the bulk-to-boundary propagator
K^{+}_{p}(\eta_{0},\eta_{i})=-K^{-}_{-p}(\eta_{0},\eta_{i})
for
p_{2}
and so on

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-1}}^{\eta_{1}\eta_{2}-\text{part}}=(i\lambda)^{2}\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\Bigg[\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\bigg)\\
&-\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},-p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},-p_{2})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},-p_{2})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},-p_{2})\bigg)\Bigg]\\
=&\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})-\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},-p_{2};p_{1})\equiv\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\,.\end{split}

(203)

The last equality is obtained from the definition of a
2
-site tree level correlator.

Therefore, the term-1 from the eq.(
200
) can finally be written as follows

{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-1}}=\frac{1}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})

(204)

Now we move to the term-2. Note that the
\eta_{3}
integral in eq.(
201
) can be written in the same token as eq.(
79
)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-2}}^{\eta_{3}-\text{part}}\\
=&(i\lambda)\int d\eta_{3}\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})-K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{3})\Big)\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{3}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\\
=&~\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p_{2})\Big)~~~~\equiv~~~~\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\end{split}

(205)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
was defined earlier in eq.(
77
).

Now we analyze the remaining part of the term-2 in eq.(
201
) involving
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
integral

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-2}}^{\eta_{1}\eta_{2}-\text{part}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{2}}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\})\bigg)\\
&\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})-K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\\
=&\frac{-(i\lambda)^{2}}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\Bigg[\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\bigg)\\
&+\bigg(p_{2}\to-p_{2}\bigg)\Bigg]\,.\end{split}

(206)

We now analyze the first two lines. But note that the combinations of the four pieces in both pair of lines is in such a way that we can’t write them in terms of a correlator. In particular the signs prevent us from writing them as in-in correlator. Therefore, one can try to write it in terms of the wave-function coefficients both at the contact and the exchange level.

In eq.(
22
) we have seen that the bulk-to-bulk propagators for in-in correlator are related to that of the wave-function coefficient in the following way

\begin{split}&G_{p_{1}}^{++}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=G_{p_{1}}^{\psi}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})+P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})K^{\psi}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})K^{\psi}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\,,\\
&G_{p_{1}}^{--}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=G_{p_{1}}^{\psi}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})^{*}+P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})K^{\psi}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})^{*}K^{\psi}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})^{*}\,.\end{split}

(207)

Using these relations, let’s first analyze the following set of terms appeared in the first two lines of eq.(
206
)

\begin{split}&(i\lambda)^{2}\int^{(2)}d\eta~\bigg(-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\\
&+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\\
&+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(208)

where the first two terms will produce the following expression

\begin{split}&-(i\lambda)^{2}\int^{(2)}d\eta\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{++}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{--}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\bigg)\\
=&-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{M}}(\eta_{0})P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})\Big(2i~\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\\
&+\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{1},p_{2})-\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})^{*}\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{1},p_{2})^{*}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(209)

And the other two terms can be written as follows

\begin{split}&-(i\lambda)^{2}\int^{(2)}d\eta\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{+-}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\\
&-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})G^{-+}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2})\bigg)\\
=&\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{M}}(\eta_{0})P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})\Big(\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{1},p_{2})^{*}\\
&-\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})^{*}\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{1},p_{2})\Big)\,.\end{split}

(210)

Combining the above two expressions we obtain the following expression which in particular corresponds to the first two lines of eq.(
206
)

\begin{split}&\frac{-2i\Bigg(\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})\times\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{1},p_{2})}{\left(2P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})\right)^{-1}}\Bigg)}{\left(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{M}}(\eta_{0})P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})\right)^{-1}}\\
&\equiv-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1}\right)\,,\end{split}

(211)

where we defined an auxiliary object at
2
-sites
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1}\right)
.
One can also write an expression for
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(3)}
as follows
33
33

33

At three site, we don’t need this expression. However, to write a single-cut rule for a 4-site tree level correlator,
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(3)}
will be necessary.

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(3)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M_{1}},\mathbf{k}_{M_{2}}\},p_{3};p_{1};p_{2}\right)=\frac{2i}{\left(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{L}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{M_{1}}}(\eta_{0})\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}_{M_{2}}}(\eta_{0})P_{p_{3}}(\eta_{0})\right)^{-1}}\times\\
&\Bigg(\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(3)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M_{1}},\mathbf{k}_{M_{2}}\},p_{3};p_{1};p_{2}\right)+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M_{1}}\},p_{2};p_{1})\times\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M_{2}}\},p_{1},p_{2})}{\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p_{2})}\\
&+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})\times\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(2)}(\{(\mathbf{k}_{M_{1}},p_{1}),(\{\mathbf{k}_{M_{2}},p_{3})\};p_{2})}{\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p_{1})}\\
&+\frac{\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})\times\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M_{1}}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{M_{2}}\},p_{2},p_{3})}{\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p_{1})~\mathbb{R}e\psi_{2}(p_{2})}\Bigg)\,.\end{split}

(212)

Notice that the auxiliary object,
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}
we have defined, has a structure very similar to a
2
-site correlator except the
\mathbb{I}m
part. With this, we finally obtain the following expression that corresponds to eq.(
206
)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-2}}^{\eta_{1}\eta_{2}-\text{part}}\\
&=-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},-p_{2};p_{1}\right)\equiv-\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1}\right)\,.\end{split}

(213)

Therefore, the term-2 from eq.(
201
) can finally be written by combining eq.(
205
) and eq.(
213
) as follows

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})\Bigg|_{\text{term-2}}=-\frac{\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\,.\end{split}

(214)

So the single-cut rule of a 3-site correlator can be expressed in the following form

\begin{split}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(3)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1};p_{2})=&\frac{1}{2P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\Big(\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\\
&-\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\Bigg)\,.\end{split}

(215)

Figure 11:
Diagrammatic representation of the single-cut rule of a
3
-site correlator as in eq.(
215
). Red circle denotes correlator
\mathcal{B}
and the purple circle denotes the auxiliary object
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
. The blue shaded line implies taking Disc with respect to the energy corresponding to that line and the yellow shaded line implies taking
\widetilde{\text{Disc}}
with respect to the energy corresponding to that line.

It turns out that for a conformally convergent polynomial interaction with
even~n
, the first piece only contributes since the second piece becomes sub-leading in
\eta_{0}
. However, for
odd~n
, both the terms contribute to the leading order in
\eta_{0}
. In the following we argue how this could be seen for a polynomial interaction
\phi^{n}
.

For a
2
-site correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(2)}
with
\phi^{n}
conformally coupled IR convergent interaction with
n=even
, the leading
\eta_{0}
piece scales as
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{2n-2}\right)
and for a contact correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}
the leading
\eta_{0}
piece scales as
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{n}\right)
. Therefore the product of their discontinuities becomes at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{3n-2}\right)
. As a result the first term in the parenthesis of the above expression has the following leading
\eta_{0}
behaviour at
\eta_{0}\to 0
limit

\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\Bigg|_{n=\text{even}}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{3n-2}\right).

(216)

Turning to the auxiliary contributions, the leading real part of the one-site wave-function coefficient
\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}
and imaginary parts of the one–site wave-function coefficient
\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}
behave as
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-n}\right)
and
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-n+1}\right)
respectively, the latter being the next–to–leading real piece. From eq.(
211
) the leading non–vanishing auxiliary contribution enters at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-2n+3}\right)
which is consistent with
\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(2)}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-2n+3}\right)
as it should be. This results in the leading
\eta_{0}
contribution of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}
contributing at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{2n-1}\right)
. Next note that the leading
\eta_{0}
behaviour of the one site auxiliary object
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
scales as
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{n+1}\right)
. Therefore the second term in the RHS of eq.(
215
) exhibits the following leading
\eta_{0}
behaviour

\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\Bigg|_{n=\text{even}}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{3n}\right)\,.

(217)

Since this is parametrically subleading relative to the first term
\mathcal{O}(\eta_{0}^{3n-2})
, we conclude that for even
n
the correlator discontinuity alone captures the full leading late–time behaviour.

We now present a similar argument for
n=odd
case. For odd interaction
2
-site correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(2)}
, contributes at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{2n-2}\right)
whereas the contact correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}
now behaves as
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{n+1}\right)
. So the product of the correlator discontinuities

\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\Bigg|_{n=\text{odd}}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{3n-1}\right)\,.

(218)

On the other hand, For the wave-function coefficients, the hierarchy is reversed relative to the even
n
case, meaning that
\mathbb{R}e\psi^{(1)}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-n+1}\right)
and
\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(1)}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-n}\right)
, with the imaginary part now providing the leading contribution. As before, the combination entering in the RHS of equation (
211
) scales as
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-2n+3}\right)
. The other term i.e. the imaginary part of the two site wave-function coefficient
\mathbb{I}m\psi^{(2)}
contributes at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{-2n+3}\right)
as it was formerly noted. This makes the leading
\eta_{0}
contribution of the
2
-site auxiliary piece
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}
appearing at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{2n-1}\right)
. Next note that the one site auxiliary object
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
contributes at
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{n}\right)
. Therefore the second term in the RHS of eq.(
215
) exhibits the following leading
\eta_{0}
behaviour

\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{M}\},p_{2};p_{1})\widetilde{\text{Disc}_{p_{2}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{2})\Bigg|_{n=\text{odd}}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(\eta_{0}^{3n-1}\right)\,.

(219)

Thus, for odd
n
, both terms in (
215
) contribute at the same order in
\eta_{0}
. In this case, retaining only the discontinuity term would miss part of the leading late–time structure, and the auxiliary contributions must be included.

Appendix E
Details of deriving the discontinuity relation for loops

In this appendix we provide additional details which we skipped in §
5.3
while deriving the cutting rule for loop diagrams. Substituting eq.(
117
) and eq.(
118
) into eq.(
116
) produces several terms, which we now analyze individually.

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 1}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{2}}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\times\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\,,\end{split}

(220)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 2}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{2}}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\times\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})-K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})-K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\,,\end{split}

(221)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 3}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{2}}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\times\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})-K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})-K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\,,\end{split}

(222)

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 4}}=\frac{(i\lambda)^{2}}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\times\int^{(2)}d\eta~\times\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})-K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})-K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\\
&\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})-K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})-K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\,.\end{split}

(223)

Now we simplify a part of the first term in eq.(
220
) as follows

\begin{split}&(i\lambda)\int d\eta_{1}\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\\
&=(i\lambda)\int d\eta_{1}\bigg(-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1})\bigg)\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\times\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})+K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\Big)\\
&=(i\lambda)\int\eta_{1}\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1},-p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1},p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},-p_{2})\\
&~~~~~~~~~~+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},-p_{2})\\
&~~~~~~~~~~+\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1},p_{2})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{1}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1},-p_{2})\bigg)\\
&=\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1},-p_{2})-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},-p_{1},p_{2})-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},-p_{2})+\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\,.\end{split}

(224)

Here the last line simply follows from the definition of the contact correlator.
The remaining contribution of eq.(
220
) can be obtained from the above result by using
\eta_{1}\leftrightarrow\eta_{2},\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\}\leftrightarrow\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\}
. Therefore,

\begin{split}&(i\lambda)\int d\eta_{2}\bigg(\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{+}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})-\mathcal{K}_{\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}^{-}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\})\bigg)\Big(K_{p_{1}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{1}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\Big(K_{p_{2}}^{+}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})+K_{p_{2}}^{-}(\eta_{0},\eta_{2})\Big)\\
&=\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p_{1},-p_{2})-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},-p_{1},p_{2})-\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{1},-p_{2})+\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{1},p_{2})\,.\end{split}

(225)

Therefore, eq.(
220
) can be written as follows

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 1}}\\
&=\frac{\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\\
&=\frac{{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{1},p_{2})}{4\,P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})\,P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\,.\end{split}

(226)

Similarly, eq.(
221
) can be written as follows

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 2}}\\
&=-\frac{\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\sigma_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\sigma_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\\
&=-\frac{{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{1},p_{2})}{4\,P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})\,P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\,.\end{split}

(227)

Similarly, eq.(
222
) can be written as follows

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 3}}\\
&=-\frac{\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\sigma_{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\sigma_{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\\
&=-\frac{\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{1}}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{1}}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{1},p_{2})}{4\,P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})\,P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\,.\end{split}

(228)

Similarly, eq.(
223
) can be written as follows

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]\Bigg|_{\text{term 4}}\\
&=\frac{\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},\sigma_{1}p_{1},\sigma_{2}p_{2})}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\\
&=\frac{\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{1}}\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{1}}\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},p_{1},p_{2})}{4\,P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})\,P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}\,.\end{split}

(229)

Combining equations eq.(
226
)- eq.(
229
) yields the following result

\begin{split}&{\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\Big[{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},p_{2})\Big]=\frac{1}{4{{P_{p_{1}}(\eta_{0})}}{{P_{p_{2}}(\eta_{0})}}}\times\\
&\Bigg[\bigg({\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times(L\to R)+\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{1}}\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times(L\to R)\bigg)\\
&-\bigg({\text{Disc}}_{p_{1}}\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times(L\to R)+\widetilde{{\text{Disc}}}_{p_{1}}{\text{Disc}}_{p_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},p_{1},p_{2})\times(L\to R)\bigg)\Bigg]\,.\end{split}

(230)

Figure 12:
Diagrammatic representation of the single-cut rule the 1-loop
2
-site correlator.

Motivated by the 1-loop cutting rule result, we propose the following cutting rule for the two-site correlator at arbitrary
\ell
-loop order.

\begin{split}&\mathrm{Disc}_{p_{\ell+1}}\cdots\mathrm{Disc}_{p_{1}}\,\mathbb{B}^{(2)}\!\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell+1}\right)=\frac{\mathcal{F}_{1}}{2^{\ell+1}\left(\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{\ell+1}P_{p_{j}}(\eta_{0})\right)}\,,\end{split}

(231)

such that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\{1,\dots,\ell+1\}\\
|S|\ \mathrm{even}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{S}\,\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\};p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell+1})\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{S}\,\mathcal{B}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell+1})\right)\\
&\qquad-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\{1,\dots,\ell+1\}\\
|S|\ \mathrm{odd}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{S}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\};p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell+1})\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{S}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\};p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell+1})\right)\,,\end{split}

(232)

where the operator
\mathcal{D}_{S}
(with the same subset
S
as in the sums above) is defined by

\mathcal{D}_{S}\;\equiv\;\left(\prod_{i\in S}\widetilde{\mathrm{Disc}}_{p_{i}}\right)\left(\prod_{i\notin S}\mathrm{Disc}_{p_{i}}\right)\,.

Here,
|S|
is just the number of elements in
S
. Each subset
S
determines which internal momenta get a
\widetilde{\mathrm{Disc}}
: if
|S|
is even,
\mathcal{D}_{S}
acts on
\mathcal{B}^{(1)}
; if odd, it acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}
.

Appendix F
Comparing our cutting rules with the existing literature and improvements

Recently, in
Donath:2024utn
, the authors have derived cutting rules for a two sites correlator, even with loop-diagrams, and expressed them directly in the context of cosmological correlator. They have considered
(L+1)
-loop and
2
-site correlator using the following conformally coupled polynomial interaction

H_{\text{int}}=\int_{\vec{x}}\frac{1}{(m+L+1)!}\phi^{m+L+1}+\int_{\vec{x}}\frac{1}{(n-m+L+1)!}\phi^{n-m+L+1}\,.

(233)

For a
n
-point
(L+1)
-loop
2
-site correlator they obtain the following formula for the cutting rule
34
34

34

Note that we have rewritten the expression using the notation we have followed throughout in this paper. At tree level, since there is no loop integration, the LHS should have the full correlator
\mathcal{B}^{(2)}
instead of
\mathbb{B}^{(2)}
which denotes the loop integrand.

\begin{split}&\mathbb{B}^{(2)}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L},\mathbf{k}_{R}\};\{p_{1};p_{2};...;p_{L+1}\}\right)+(-1)^{n}\mathbb{B}^{(2)}\left(\{-\mathbf{k}_{L},-\mathbf{k}_{R}\};\{p_{1};p_{2};...;p_{L+1}\}\right)\\
&=2\left(\frac{\mathcal{B}^{(1),\text{cut}}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\},\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{L+1}\right)\mathcal{B}^{(1),\text{cut}}\left(\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\},\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{L+1}\right)}{\prod_{i=1}^{L+1}P_{p_{i}}(\eta_{0})}\right)\,,\end{split}

(234)

where,
n
is the total number of external lines and according to our notation
\{\mathbf{k}_{L}\}
is the collection of external lines labelled by
\{k_{1},k_{2},...,k_{m}\}
whereas
\{\mathbf{k}_{R}\}=\{k_{m+1},k_{m+2},...,k_{n}\}
.

\mathcal{B}^{(1),\text{cut}}\left(\{\mathbf{k}\},\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{L+1}\right)
is defined by the following expression
